头一夜我们俩都乏透了，她尤其乏。我们没说几句话，她一吃完，我便打发她上床睡了。第二天一早，我先写信给斯普罗特，叫他把她的行李寄来，又给在族长那儿的艾伦捎了短短一行；信发出去、她的早饭备好了，我才叫醒她。她走出来，身上还是那一件衣裳，袜子上带着一路的泥，我瞧着有点窘。照我打听的，她的行李要好些天才能到莱顿；明摆着她得有换洗的衣物。起先她不肯叫我破费；我提醒她，她如今是一位阔人的妹妹，场面得相称；还没逛到第二家铺子，她已经整个儿迷在这桩事里，眼睛发亮了。看她这样天真、这样全心全意地快活，我心里欢喜。更古怪的是我自己也落进去的那股热乎劲：总嫌给她买得不够多、不够好，看她换一身行头就看不厌。真的，格兰特小姐沉在衣裳那个兴趣里，我开始懂得一点了；因为实情是，你手里既然有一位美人打底子，这桩营生整个儿就成了美的。荷兰的印花布，我得说，又贱又好得出奇；可给她买长袜花了多少，我羞于记下。这一趟买欢——不妨这样叫——我花的钱太多，好些日子都不好意思再花；作为找补，我们那两间屋便布置得光光溜溜。

===Original===

If we had beds, if Catriona was a little braw, and I had light to see her by, we were richly enough lodged for me.
By the end of this merchandising I was glad to leave her at the door with all our purchases, and go for a long walk alone in which to read myself a lecture. Here had I taken under my roof, and as good as to my bosom, a young lass extremely beautiful, and whose innocence was her peril. My talk with the old Dutchman, and the lies to which I was constrained, had already given me a sense of how my conduct must appear to others; and now, after the strong admiration I had just experienced and the immoderacy with which I had continued my vain purchases, I began to think of it myself as very hasarded. I bethought me, if I had a sister indeed, whether I would so expose her; then, judging the case too problematical, I varied my question into this, whether I would so trust Catriona in the hands of any other Christian being: the answer to which made my face to burn. The more cause, since I had been entrapped and had entrapped the girl into an undue situation, that I should behave in it with scrupulous nicety. She depended on me wholly for her bread and shelter; in case I should alarm her delicacy, she had no retreat. Besides, I was her host and her protector; and the more irregularly I had fallen in these positions, the less excuse for me if I should profit by the same to forward even the most honest suit; for with the opportunities that I enjoyed, and which no wise parent would have suffered for a moment, even the most honest suit would be unfair. I saw I must be extremely hold-off in my relations; and yet not too much so neither; for if I had no right to appear at all in the character of a suitor, I must yet appear continually, and if possible agreeably, in that of host. It was plain I should require a great deal of tact and conduct, perhaps more than my years afforded. But I had rushed in where angels might have feared to tread, and there was no way out of that position, save by behaving right while I was in it. I made a set of rules for my guidance; prayed for strength to be enabled to observe them, and as a more human aid to the same end purchased a study book in law.

===Chinese===

只要有床，只要卡特琳娜略微穿得光鲜，只要我有灯照得见她，我住得就算很阔气了。
这趟采买做完，我把她连人带货物撂在门口，独自去走一场长长的路，路上给自己讲一篇道理。我这是把一个极标致的姑娘收在我的屋檐下，收得几乎贴在心口上，而她的天真正是她的危险。同那荷兰老人的谈话，以及我不得不说的那些谎，已经叫我知道我的行径落在旁人眼里是什么样子；如今又加上方才那阵强烈的倾心、那阵不知节制的胡乱采买，我自己也开始觉得这事很悬。我暗想：倘若我真有个妹妹，我会这样把她示人么？转念又觉得这问法难说，便换个问法：卡特琳娜，我会把她这样托在任何别的教徒手里么？这答案叫我的脸烧起来。正因为我是受了圈套、又拉着这姑娘落进一个不合规矩的境地，我在里头行事才更该一板一眼、分毫不苟。她的吃住全仗我一人；她的娇羞若叫我惊着，她没有退路。何况我是她的东道、她的保护人；我落在这两个位分上本已不正，再想借它们之便去推进哪怕最诚实的求婚，我就更没有借口了；因为凭我享着的这些便宜——任何一个明白的家长连一刻也不会容许的——连最诚实的求婚也是不公道的。我明白，同她的来往我必须极有分寸；可又不能太有分寸；因为我既没有权利以求婚人的身份露头角，却又要时时刻刻、还得尽可能讨人喜欢地以东道的身份露面。这明摆着要我极多的机变和涵养，兴许还多过我那个年纪。可我已经闯进了天使都要却步的地方；这处境没有第二条出路，只有在里头行得正。我给自己订了一套规矩；祷告给我守规矩的力量；又作为更人间的助力，买了一部法学教科书。

===Original===

This being all that I could think of, I relaxed from these grave considerations; whereupon my mind bubbled at once into an effervescency of pleasing spirits, and it was like one treading on air that I turned homeward. As I thought that name of home, and recalled the image of that figure awaiting me between four walls, my heart beat upon my bosom.
My troubles began with my return. She ran to greet me with an obvious and affecting pleasure. She was clad, besides, entirely in the new clothes that I had bought for her; looked in them beyond expression well; and must walk about and drop me curtseys to display them and to be admired. I am sure I did it with an ill grace, for I thought to have choked upon the words.
“Well,” she said, “if you will not be caring for my pretty clothes, see what I have done with our two chambers.” And she showed me the place all very finely swept and the fires glowing in the two chimneys.
I was glad of a chance to seem a little more severe than I quite felt. “Catriona,” said I, “I am very much displeased with you, and you must never again lay a hand upon my room. One of us two must have the rule while we are here together; it is most fit it should be I who am both the man and the elder; and I give you that for my command.”
She dropped me one of her curtseys which were extraordinary taking. “If you will be cross,” said she, “I must be making pretty manners at you, Davie. I will be very obedient, as I should be when every stitch upon all there is of me belongs to you. But you will not be very cross either, because now I have not anyone else.”
This struck me hard, and I made haste, in a kind of penitence, to blot out all the good effect of my last speech. In this direction, progress was more easy, being down hill; she led me forward, smiling; at the sight of her, in the brightness of the fire and with her pretty becks and looks, my heart was altogether melted. We made our meal with infinite mirth and tenderness; and the two seemed to be commingled into one, so that our very laughter sounded like a kindness.
In the midst of which I awoke to better recollections, made a lame word of excuse, and set myself boorishly to my studies. It was a substantial, instructive book that I had bought, by the late Dr.

===Chinese===

能想到的都想尽了，我便从这些沉甸甸的思虑里松下来；心一下子沸腾起一派欢快的情绪，回家的一路像踩在空气上。想到「家」这个字，想起那道身影在四堵墙里等我的样子，我的心跳在胸口上。
我的烦恼随我进门开始。她跑上来迎我，那欢喜明明白白、动人极了。她还穿了一身我买给她的新衣裳，穿着好看得没法说；偏要走来走去、朝我屈膝行礼，好叫我看、叫我夸。我夸得实在勉强，话哽在嗓子里差点出不来。
「好嘛，」她说，「你既然不心疼我的漂亮衣裳，就瞧瞧我把咱们两间屋子收拾成了什么样。」她指给我看：屋子扫得干干净净，两只烟囱里火苗正旺。
我乐得有个机会，摆得比心里实际更板正些。「卡特琳娜，」我说，「我很不痛快；我的屋子，你再不许动一根手指头。咱们俩住在一处，总得有一个说了算；我是男的，又年长，理当是我；这话你就当我的命令。」
她朝我行了一个屈膝礼——她那屈膝礼好看得出奇。「你要板脸，」她说，「我就只好跟你讲礼貌了，戴维。我一定乖乖的，也本该乖乖的——我浑身上下每一针每一线都是你的呢。可你也别太板脸，因为我如今再没有别的人了。」
这话扎得我不轻；我慌忙带着一股悔意，把上一篇话的好效果全数抹掉。往这个方向走容易得多——一路下坡；她笑吟吟领着我往前走；望着她，映着火光，带着她那好看的屈膝和眼色，我的心整个儿化了。我们吃饭吃得无限欢喜无限温存；两样仿佛并成了一样，连笑声听上去都像一种恩爱。
正到其间，我惊醒过来，含含糊糊告了个罪，粗声粗气坐下来用功。我买的那是一部扎实有益的书，已故的海内克齐乌斯——

===Original===

Heineccius, in which I was to do a great deal of reading these next days, and often very glad that I had no one to question me of what I read. Methought she bit her lip at me a little, and that cut me. Indeed it left her wholly solitary, the more as she was very little of a reader, and had never a book. But what was I to do?
So the rest of the evening flowed by almost without speech.
I could have beat myself. I could not lie in my bed that night for rage and repentance, but walked to and fro on my bare feet till I was nearly perished, for the chimney was gone out and the frost keen. The thought of her in the next room, the thought that she might even hear me as I walked, the remembrance of my churlishness and that I must continue to practise the same ungrateful course or be dishonoured, put me beside my reason. I stood like a man between Scylla and Charybdis: What must she think of me? was my one thought that softened me continually into weakness. What is to become of us? the other which steeled me again to resolution. This was my first night of wakefulness and divided counsels, of which I was now to pass many, pacing like a madman, sometimes weeping like a childish boy, sometimes praying (I would fain hope) like a Christian.
But prayer is not very difficult, and the hitch comes in practice. In her presence, and above all if I allowed any beginning of familiarity, I found I had very little command of what should follow. But to sit all day in the same room with her, and feign to be engaged upon Heineccius, surpassed my strength. So that I fell instead upon the expedient of absenting myself so much as I was able; taking out classes and sitting there regularly, often with small attention, the test of which I found the other day in a notebook of that period, where I had left off to follow an edifying lecture and actually scribbled in my book some very ill verses, though the Latinity is rather better than I thought I could ever have compassed. The evil of this course was unhappily near as great as its advantage. I had the less time of trial, but I believe, while that time lasted, I was tried the more extremely. For she being so much left to solitude, she came to greet my return with an increasing fervour that came nigh to overmaster me.

===Chinese===

——博士的著作；往后这些天我在里头要读上许多，还常常暗自庆幸没人来考我读的什么。我觉得她朝我轻轻咬了咬嘴唇，那叫我心口一疼。这实在是把她整个儿撇下了，何况她又不大读书，手边一本书也没有。可叫我怎么办呢？
那天晚上余下的时光，便几乎在无言中流了过去。
我恨不得打自己。那天夜里我气恼交加、悔恨交加，躺不住床，赤着脚在屋里来回地走，几乎冻僵——烟囱已经熄了，霜气正紧。一想到她在隔壁屋里，一想到我走路的声音她说不定听得见，一想起我的粗鲁，又想起这副忘恩的路数我还得继续走下去、否则便失了体面，我就几乎失了理性。我像一个人站在斯库拉和卡律布狄斯之间：「她心里把我看成什么人？」这一个念头，时时刻刻把我软化成软弱；「往后我们怎么办？」那一个念头，又把我重新炼成决断。这是我头一夜的失眠与交战；这样的夜往后我过了许多——像疯子一样踱步，有时像小孩子一样哭，有时（我但愿如此）像基督徒一样祷告。
可祷告不难，难的是实行。当着她的面，尤其容得半分亲热起了头，我就几乎管不住后头的事。可是同她在一间屋里坐上一整天、装作埋头海内克齐乌斯，又超出我的力气。于是我改用一条权宜之计：尽量躲出去；报名上些课，按钟点坐在那里，多半心不在焉——证据是前几天我在那个时期的一个笔记本里翻见的：一堂有益的课上到一半，我不听讲了，竟在本子上涂了几句很不像样的诗，虽说那拉丁文比我自以为能写出的高明些。这条路子的害处，不幸同好处一样大。受磨的时候少了；可我相信，熬着的当儿，我熬得格外凶。因为她那样被撇在冷清里，迎我回来时的那股热乎劲一天浓似一天，几乎要把我也淹了。

===Original===

These friendly offers I must barbarously cast back; and my rejection sometimes wounded her so cruelly that I must unbend and seek to make it up to her in kindness. So that our time passed in ups and downs, tiffs and disappointments, upon the which I could almost say (if it may be said with reverence) that I was crucified.
The base of my trouble was Catriona’s extraordinary innocence, at which I was not so much surprised as filled with pity and admiration. She seemed to have no thought of our position, no sense of my struggles; welcomed any mark of my weakness with responsive joy; and when I was drove again to my retrenchments, did not always dissemble her chagrin. There were times when I have thought to myself, “If she were over head in love, and set her cap to catch me, she would scarce behave much otherwise;” and then I would fall again into wonder at the simplicity of woman, from whom I felt (in these moments) that I was not worthy to be descended.
There was one point in particular on which our warfare turned, and of all things, this was the question of her clothes. My baggage had soon followed me from Rotterdam, and hers from Helvoet. She had now, as it were, two wardrobes; and it grew to be understood between us (I could never tell how) that when she was friendly she would wear my clothes, and when otherwise her own. It was meant for a buffet, and (as it were) the renunciation of her gratitude; and I felt it so in my bosom, but was generally more wise than to appear to have observed the circumstance.
Once, indeed, I was betrayed into a childishness greater than her own; it fell in this way. On my return from classes, thinking upon her devoutly with a great deal of love and a good deal of annoyance in the bargain, the annoyance began to fade away out of my mind; and spying in a window one of those forced flowers, of which the Hollanders are so skilled in the artifice, I gave way to an impulse and bought it for Catriona. I do not know the name of that flower, but it was of the pink colour, and I thought she would admire the same, and carried it home to her with a wonderful soft heart.

===Chinese===

这些好意我只好野蛮地挡回去；有时挡得太狠，伤了她，我又得软下来，用温存找补。我们的日子便这样起起落落、呕气、失望地过着；这滋味（若容我不敬地说）几乎像是一场钉十字架。
我烦恼的根子，是卡特琳娜那份出奇的天真；我对它与其说诧异，不如说满心怜惜又敬佩。她仿佛全不想想我们的处境，全觉不出我的挣扎；我每露一点软弱的形迹，她便报以欢喜；我再缩回去，她也不总掩饰她的委屈。有几回我心里想：「她若是情窦初开、存心拿网来罩我，也未必就比这更像；」跟着我又惊叹起女人的单纯来——在那种时刻，我觉得自己不配做这单纯的女子的后代。
有一处特别是我们交战的焦点；说来奇怪，偏偏是她衣裳的问题。我的行李早从鹿特丹跟了来，她的也从海尔沃特到了。她如今等于有两份衣裳；我们之间渐渐形成了这么一条默契（怎么形成的，我说不上）：她跟我亲热，便穿我买的；不亲热，便穿她自己的。这是一记耳光，是（好比说）撤回她的感激；我心里感觉得到，可多半装傻，只当不曾留意。
有一回，我真的失陷进比她更孩子气的孩子气里；事情是这样的。那天我从课堂回来，一心虔敬地想着她，满腔的爱，也满腔的恼；恼渐渐从心里退了；路过一家橱窗，看见一枝催开的花——荷兰人最精这门巧艺——我一冲动，便买了给卡特琳娜。那花叫什么名字我不知道，是粉色的；我想她会喜欢，揣着一颗出奇软和的心带回家去给她。

===Original===

I had left her in my clothes, and when I returned to find her all changed and a face to match, I cast but the one look at her from head to foot, ground my teeth together, flung the window open, and my flower into the court, and then (between rage and prudence) myself out of that room again, of which I slammed the door as I went out.
On the steep stair I came near falling, and this brought me to myself, so that I began at once to see the folly of my conduct. I went, not into the street as I had purposed, but to the house court, which was always a solitary place, and where I saw my flower (that had cost me vastly more than it was worth) hanging in the leafless tree. I stood by the side of the canal, and looked upon the ice. Country people went by on their skates, and I envied them. I could see no way out of the pickle I was in: no way so much as to return to the room I had just left. No doubt was in my mind but I had now betrayed the secret of my feelings; and to make things worse, I had shown at the same time (and that with wretched boyishness) incivility to my helpless guest.
I suppose she must have seen me from the open window. It did not seem to me that I had stood there very long before I heard the crunching of footsteps on the frozen snow, and turning somewhat angrily (for I was in no spirit to be interrupted) saw Catriona drawing near. She was all changed again, to the clocked stockings.
“Are we not to have our walk today?” said she.
I was looking at her in a maze. “Where is your brooch?” says I.
She carried her hand to her bosom and coloured high. “I will have forgotten it,” said she. “I will run upstairs for it quick, and then surely we’ll can have our walk?”
There was a note of pleading in that last that staggered me; I had neither words nor voice to utter them; I could do no more than nod by way of answer; and the moment she had left me, climbed into the tree and recovered my flower, which on her return I offered her.
“I bought it for you, Catriona,” said I.
She fixed it in the midst of her bosom with the brooch, I could have thought tenderly.
“It is none the better of my handling,” said I again, and blushed.
“I will be liking it none the worse, you may be sure of that,” said she.
We did not speak so much that day, she seemed a thought on the reserve though not unkindly.

===Chinese===

我出门时她穿着我的衣裳；回来一见她整个儿换掉了，脸色也换了。我从头到脚只扫了她一眼，牙关一咬，推开窗子，把我的花扔进院子，随即（在暴怒和谨慎之间）自己也退出那间屋子，出门时把门砰地带上。
陡楼梯上我险些跌一跤；这一跌倒把我跌醒了，登时看出自己行径的荒唐。我没有照原打算上街，而是进了那方院子——那里向来清静——望见我的花（花去我的钱远超它值得的价钱）挂在秃树上。我立在运河边，望着冰。乡下人穿着冰鞋来来去去，我羡慕他们。我看不出我这个窘境的出路：连回方才那间屋的路都没有。我心里毫无疑义：我的心思这一下全露了；更糟的是，我同时还（以那样恶劣的孩子气）慢待了我这无依无靠的客人。
她多半是从敞着的窗口望见了我。我在那里仿佛还没有站多久，便听得冻雪上咯吱咯吱的脚步声；我一回头，带着几分火气（我没心思让人搅扰），见卡特琳娜走来。她又整个儿换回来了，换上了绣花长袜。
「咱们今天不散步了么？」她说。
我痴痴地望着她。「你的胸针呢？」我说。
她一手按住胸口，脸飞红。「我忘了它，」她说，「我这就跑上去拿，拿了咱们准定能散步去？」
那最后一句里的恳求调子叫我把持不住；我没有话，也没有嗓音；只会点头作答；她一走，我立刻爬上树取回我的花，等她回来便献给她。
「这是给你买的，卡特琳娜。」我说。
她用胸针把它别在胸口正中，那动作我几乎觉得温存。
「经我这一拿，它不好看了。」我又说，脸红了。
「它也不见得就差了，这个你放心。」她说。
那天我们说话不多；她像是存了点戒心，倒不见得不亲热。

===Original===

As for me, all the time of our walking, and after we came home, and I had seen her put my flower into a pot of water, I was thinking to myself what puzzles women were. I was thinking, the one moment, it was the most stupid thing on earth she should not have perceived my love; and the next, that she had certainly perceived it long ago, and (being a wise girl with the fine female instinct of propriety) concealed her knowledge.
We had our walk daily. Out in the streets I felt more safe; I relaxed a little in my guardedness; and for one thing, there was no Heineccius. This made these periods not only a relief to myself, but a particular pleasure to my poor child. When I came back about the hour appointed, I would generally find her ready dressed and glowing with anticipation. She would prolong their duration to the extreme, seeming to dread (as I did myself) the hour of the return; and there is scarce a field or waterside near Leyden, scarce a street or lane there, where we have not lingered. Outside of these, I bade her confine herself entirely to our lodgings; this in the fear of her encountering any acquaintance, which would have rendered our position very difficult. From the same apprehension I would never suffer her to attend church, nor even go myself; but made some kind of shift to hold worship privately in our own chamber﻿—I hope with an honest, but I am quite sure with a very much divided mind. Indeed, there was scarce anything that more affected me, than thus to kneel down alone with her before God like man and wife.
One day it was snowing downright hard. I had thought it not possible that we should venture forth, and was surprised to find her waiting for me ready dressed.
“I will not be doing without my walk,” she cried. “You are never a good boy, Davie, in the house; I will never be caring for you only in the open air. I think we two will better turn Egyptian and dwell by the roadside.”
That was the best walk yet of all of them; she clung near to me in the falling snow; it beat about and melted on us, and the drops stood upon her bright cheeks like tears and ran into her smiling mouth. Strength seemed to come upon me with the sight like a giant’s; I thought I could have caught her up and run with her into the uttermost places in the earth; and we spoke together all that time beyond belief for freedom and sweetness.

===Chinese===

至于我，散步一路，回家以后望见她把我的花插进一瓶水里——我心里一直在想：女人真是谜。这一刻我想，天下再没有比这更傻的事了：她竟没看出我的爱；下一刻又想，她分明早就看出来了，只是（她是个明白姑娘，有女人对分寸的天然直觉）把知道了的藏起来罢了。
我们天天散步。在外头街上我觉得稳当些，戒备松了一分；一来，海内克齐乌斯不在手边。这便使这些时辰不光是我松一口气，也成了我那可怜的孩子一份特别的乐事。到了约定的钟点我回来，多半见她早已穿戴停当，满脸放光地盼着。她总要拖到不能再拖，仿佛（同我一样）怕那回去的时辰；莱顿左近的田野、水边，那里的街街巷巷，没有一处我们不曾流连。除这些之外，我叫她全然守在寓所里，生怕她撞见熟人，把我们的处境弄得为难。为着同一个顾虑，我从不让她上教堂，连我自己也不去；只在自家屋里想法子做礼拜——但愿是诚心的，可我敢说心分成了好几瓣。真的，再没有几件事比这更摇撼我：同她两个，就这样像夫妻一样跪在上帝面前。
有一天，雪下得实在紧。我原想我们不能出门了，却惊讶地见她穿戴齐整等着我。
「我少不了我的散步，」她嚷道，「在屋里你从来不是个好孩子，戴维；只有在外头我才是喜欢你的。我看咱们两个不如学埃及人，在路边安家。」
那是所有散步里最好的一回；落雪里她紧紧挨着我；雪片扑打在我们身上化了，水珠立在她光亮的脸蛋上，像眼泪，淌进她微笑的嘴里。一见这光景，一股巨人的力气仿佛上身；我想我可以把她抱起来，抱着她跑到地角天边；那一整段时间我们说的话，那份自在、那份甜，简直不能叫人相信。

===Original===

It was the dark night when we came to the house door. She pressed my arm upon her bosom. “Thank you kindly for these same good hours,” said she, on a deep note of her voice.
The concern in which I fell instantly on this address, put me with the same swiftness on my guard; and we were no sooner in the chamber, and the light made, than she beheld the old, dour, stubborn countenance of the student of Heineccius. Doubtless she was more than usually hurt; and I know for myself, I found it more than usually difficult to maintain my strangeness. Even at the meal, I durst scarce unbuckle and scarce lift my eyes to her; and it was no sooner over than I fell again to my civilian, with more seeming abstraction and less understanding than before. Methought, as I-read, I could hear my heart strike like an eight-day clock. Hard as I feigned to study, there was still some of my eyesight that spilled beyond the book upon Catriona. She sat on the floor by the side of my great mail, and the chimney lighted her up, and shone and blinked upon her, and made her glow and darken through a wonder of fine hues. Now she would be gazing in the fire, and then again at me; and at that I would be plunged in a terror of myself, and turn the pages of Heineccius like a man looking for the text in church.
Suddenly she called out aloud, “O, why does not my father come?” she cried, and fell at once into a storm of tears.
I leaped up, flung Heineccius fairly into the fire, ran to her side, and cast an arm around her sobbing body.
She put me from her sharply. “You do not love your friend,” says she. “I could be so happy too, if you would let me!” And then, “O, what will I have done that you should hate me so?”
“Hate you!” cries I, and held her firm. “You blind lass, can you not see a little in my wretched heart? Do you think when I set there, reading in that fool-book that I have just burned and be damned to it, I take ever the least thought of any stricken thing but just yourself? Night after night I could have grat to see you sitting there your lone. And what was I to do? You are here under my honour; would you punish me for that? Is it for that that you would spurn a loving servant?”
At the word, with a small, sudden motion, she clung near to me. I raised her face to mine, I kissed it, and she bowed her brow upon my bosom, clasping me tight. I sat in a mere whirl like a man drunken.

===Chinese===

到家门口，已是黑天。她把我的胳膊往胸口按了按。「多谢你这几个好时辰。」她说，声音低低的。
这一声称呼叫我登时落进的慌乱，又以同样的速度叫我警觉；一进屋、一点上灯，她见到的便是海内克齐乌斯的学生那张旧日的、又硬又倔的脸。无疑她伤得比往常更重；我自己也觉得，这回的冷淡比往常更难撑。连吃饭我都不敢松开、不敢抬眼看她；饭一完，我又埋头我的法学家，装的出神比先前更甚，懂的比先前更少。我一面读，仿佛听得自己的心像一座八日钟那样喋喋地敲。任凭我装得怎样用功，总有一点目光溢出书页、落在卡特琳娜身上。她坐在我的大行李箱旁边地板上，炉火照着她，在她身上闪呀眨呀，映得她一阵亮一阵暗，透出种种好看的颜色。她一会儿盯着火，一会儿又望着我；她一望，我便堕进对自身的恐惧里，翻海内克齐乌斯的书页，像教堂里找经文的人。
忽然她高声喊出来：「哦，我父亲怎么还不来？」喊完登时泪如雨下。
我跳起来，把海内克齐乌斯整个儿扔进火里，奔到她身边，一条胳膊搂住她抽泣的身子。
她把我猛地推开。「你不爱你的朋友，」她说，「你若容得我，我也本可以快活的！」跟着又是：「哦，我做了什么，你要这样恨我？」
「恨你！」我喊道，把她紧紧抱定。「你这个瞎姑娘，就瞧不见一点我这颗可怜的心？你以为我坐在那里，读那本傻瓜书——那本我刚烧了、罪有应得的书——的时候，心里除了你这个人，还想过别的什么倒霉事么？一夜又一夜，看你孤零零坐在那里，我差点哭出来。可叫我怎么办？你在我这里，押在我的名誉上；你要为这个罚我么？你为这个就要踢开一个爱你的仆人么？」
听到这一句，她一个细小的、突然的动作，偎进我怀里。我捧起她的脸，吻了它；她的额头伏在我胸口，把我抱得紧紧的。我坐在一阵眩晕里，像个喝醉的人。

===Original===

Then I heard her voice sound very small and muffled in my clothes.
“Did you kiss her truly?” she asked.
There went through me so great a heave of surprise that I was all shook with it.
“Miss Grant!” I cried, all in a disorder. “Yes, I asked her to kiss me goodbye, the which she did.”
“Ah, well!” said she, “you have kissed me too, at all events.”
At the strangeness and sweetness of that word, I saw where we had fallen; rose, and set her on her feet.
“This will never do,” said I. “This will never, never do. O Catrine, Catrine!” Then there came a pause in which I was debarred from any speaking. And then, “Go away to your bed,” said I. “Go away to your bed and leave me.”
She turned to obey me like a little child, and the next I knew of it, had stopped in the very doorway.
“Good night, Davie!” said she.
“And O, good night, my love!” I cried, with a great outbreak of my soul, and caught her to me again, so that it seemed I must have broken her. The next moment I had thrust her from the room, shut to the door even with violence, and stood alone.
The milk was spilt now, the word was out and the truth told. I had crept like an untrusty man into the poor maid’s affections; she was in my hand like any frail, innocent thing to make or mar; and what weapon of defence was left me? It seemed like a symbol that Heinoccius, my old protection, was now burned. I repented, yet could not find it in my heart to blame myself for that great failure. It seemed not possible to have resisted the boldness of her innocence or that last temptation of her weeping. And all that I had to excuse me did but make my sin appear the greater﻿—it was upon a nature so defenceless, and with such advantages of the position, that I seemed to have practised.
What was to become of us now? It seemed we could no longer dwell in the one place. But where was I to go? or where she? Without either choice or fault of ours, life had conspired to wall us together in that narrow place. I had a wild thought of marrying out of hand; and the next moment put it from me with revolt. She was a child, she could not tell her own heart; I had surprised her weakness, I must never go on to build on that surprisal; I must keep her not only clear of reproach, but free as she had come to me.
Down I sat before the fire, and reflected, and repented, and beat my brains in vain for any means of escape.

===Chinese===

随后我听见她的声音，闷在我的衣裳里，细小得很。
「你当真吻过她么？」她问。
一阵那么大的惊涛从我身上滚过，我整个人都摇动了。
「格兰特小姐！」我喊道，全乱了套。「是的——我请她吻别，她吻了。」
「啊，好嘛！」她说，「反正你也吻了我。」
这一个字眼那样古怪、那样甜；我猛然看清我们落到了哪里，站起来，扶她站直。
「这样不行，」我说，「这样万万不行。哦，卡特琳，卡特琳！」跟着是一阵说不出话的沉默。然后是：「回你屋里睡去，」我说，「回你屋里睡去，让我一个人。」
她像个小孩子一样转身服从；下一刻，我知道的，她已停在门槛上。
「晚安，戴维！」她说。
「哦，晚安，我的爱人！」我喊道，灵魂里一阵大决堤，又把她搂过来，搂得几乎要折断她。下一刻我已把她推出门外，砰地关上门，独自站着。
牛奶泼了；话说出了口，真相也揭了。我像一个靠不住的人，偷偷爬进那可怜姑娘的情意里；她攋在我手里，像一件易碎的、无辜的东西，成之毁之都在我；我还有什么防身的家伙？海内克齐乌斯——我的旧日的屏障——如今烧掉了，像是应景的象征。我悔恨，却狠不下心责备自己那场大失守。她那股天真的大胆、她最后那一场哭的诱惑，仿佛没法抵抗。而我所有可以开脱的情节，反倒把我的罪显得更大——我下的手，是在那样不设防的天性上，占着位分上那样的便宜。
如今我们怎么办？看来我们再不能住在一处了。可我去哪里？她去哪里？不由我们挑、也不由我们错，生活设了局，把我们两个圈死在这方寸之地。我起过一个荒唐的念头：索性立刻成婚；下一刻又厌恶地撇开。她还是个孩子，她认不得自己的心；我偷袭了她的软弱，我决不能在这偷袭上头再打主意；我不但要保她清白无损，还要保她自由，自由得同她来我这里时一样。
我在火堆前坐下，反思，悔恨，抓耳挠腮想一条出路，白想。

===Original===

About two of the morning, there were three red embers left and the house and all the city was asleep, when I was aware of a small sound of weeping in the next room. She thought that I slept, the poor soul; she regretted her weakness﻿—and what perhaps (God help her!) she called her forwardness﻿—and in the dead of the night solaced herself with tears. Tender and bitter feelings, love and penitence and pity struggled in my soul; it seemed I was under bond to heal that weeping.
“O, try to forgive me!” I cried out, “try, try to forgive me. Let us forget it all, let us try if we’ll no can forget it!”
There came no answer, but the sobbing ceased. I stood a long while with my hands still clasped as I had spoken; then the cold of the night laid hold upon me with a shudder, and I think my reason reawakened.
“You can make no hand of this, Davie,” thinks I. “To bed with you like a wise lad, and try if you can sleep. Tomorrow you may see your way.”

===Chinese===

凌晨两点钟光景，火里只剩三粒红炭，屋子、全城都睡熟了，我忽然听见隔壁屋里一阵低低的哭声。她当我睡着了呢，那可怜的人；她懊悔她的软弱——懊悔那件（上帝保佑她！）她说不定叫做她的轻佻的事——在死寂的深夜里拿眼泪安慰自己。温柔的、苦涩的种种情感，爱、悔、怜，在我心里争斗；我仿佛负着债，非医好那哭声不可。
「哦，想法子饷了我！」我喊出声，「想法子，想法子饷了我。咱们把它全忘了吧，咱们试试能不能把它全忘了！」
没有回答，只是抽泣停了。我站了很久很久，双手还保持着说话时合十的样子；后来夜寒一抖，把我攋住，我想我的理性也醒了。
「这样下去你一点辙也没有，戴维，」我暗想，「像个体面小伙子那样上床去，试试睡得着睡不着。明天，你兴许就看得出路了。」

## XIII Gillane Sands / 十三、吉兰沙滩
===Original===

I did not profit by Alan’s pilotage as he had done by his marchings under General Cope; for I can scarce tell what way we went. It is my excuse that we travelled exceeding fast. Some part we ran, some trotted, and the rest walked at a vengeance of a pace. Twice, while we were at top speed, we ran against countryfolk; but though we plumped into the first from round a corner, Alan was as ready as a loaded musket.
“Hae ye seen my horse?” he gasped.
“Na, man, I haenae seen nae horse the day,” replied the countryman.
And Alan spared the time to explain to him that we were travelling “ride and tie”; that our charger had escaped, and it was feared he had gone home to Linton. Not only that, but he expended some breath (of which he had not very much left) to curse his own misfortune and my stupidity which was said to be its cause.
“Them that cannae tell the truth,” he observed to myself as we went on again, “should be aye mindfu’ to leave an honest, handy lee behind them. If folk dinnae ken what ye’re doing, Davie, they’re terrible taken up with it; but if they think they ken, they care nae mair for it than what I do for pease porridge.”
As we had first made inland, so our road came in the end to lie very near due north; the old Kirk of Aberlady for a landmark on the left; on the right, the top of the Berwick Law; and it was thus we struck the shore again, not far from Dirleton. From North Berwick west to Gillane Ness there runs a string of four small islets, Craiglieth, the Lamb, Fidra, and Eyebrough, notable by their diversity of size and shape. Fidra is the most particular, being a strange grey islet of two humps, made the more conspicuous by a piece of ruin; and I mind that (as we drew closer to it) by some door or window of these ruins the sea peeped through like a man’s eye. Under the lee of Fidra there is a good anchorage in westerly winds, and there, from a far way off, we could see the *Thistle* riding.
The shore in face of these islets is altogether waste. Here is no dwelling of man, and scarce any passage, or at most of vagabond children running at their play. Gillane is a small place on the far side of the Ness, the folk of Dirleton go to their business in the inland fields, and those of North Berwick straight to the sea-fishing from their haven; so that few parts of the coast are lonelier.

===Chinese===

艾伦这趟领路，我可没沾着什么光，不像他当年在科珀将军麾下行军那样长见识；因为我们走的什么路，我几乎说不上来。我的辩解是：我们走得实在太快。有的路段是跑，有的是小跑，其余的也是玩命地走。全速前进时，我们两次撞上乡人；但头一回虽是拐过墙角一头撞个正着，艾伦却像上好膛的火枪一样张口就来。

「瞧见俺的马没有？」他喘着气问。

「没瞧见，伙计，今儿个没见着什么马。」乡人答道。

艾伦居然还有工夫向他解释：我们这是「换马联骑」赶路，坐骑跑丢了，只怕自己回林顿去了。不止如此，他还费了些本就不多的气息，咒自己的倒运，捎带咒我的愚蠢——据说就是这愚蠢惹的祸。

「那些不会讲实话的人，」我们重新上路时他对我说，「就该时刻记得，身后留一个诚实又顺手的谎。人家要是不知道你在干什么，戴维，那个上心哟；可人家要是自以为知道，那份不上心，就跟俺对豌豆粥一样。」

我们先头既是往内地去的，末了这条路几乎正朝北：左边以阿伯莱迪老教堂作方位，右边是贝里克劳山头；我们就是这样在迪尔顿附近重新摸到海边的。从北贝里克往西到吉兰角，一连串卧着四个小岛：克雷格利斯、羊羔岛、菲德拉和艾布拉，大小形状各异，一望可辨。菲德拉最别致，灰色小岛生着两个驼峰，又添一截废墟，越显眼；我记得（我们挨近时）那废墟的某个门洞或窗洞里，海水从后面窥出来，像人的一只眼。菲德拉背风面在西风天是个好锚地，远远地我们就望见「蓟号」泊在那里。

这几个岛对面的海岸全然荒凉：没有人烟，罕有人迹，至多有几个野孩子跑着玩。吉兰是海角那头的小地方，迪尔顿的人下地干活在内地，北贝里克的人出了海港直奔渔场：这段海岸，少有比它更荒僻的了。

===Original===

But I mind, as we crawled upon our bellies into that multiplicity of heights and hollows, keeping a bright eye upon all sides, and our hearts hammering at our ribs, there was such a shining of the sun and the sea, such a stir of the wind in the bent grass, and such a bustle of down-popping rabbits and up-flying gulls, that the desert seemed to me like a place alive. No doubt it was in all ways well chosen for a secret embarcation, if the secret had been kept; and even now that it was out, and the place watched, we were able to creep unperceived to the front of the sandhills, where they look down immediately on the beach and sea.
But here Alan came to a full stop.
“Davie,” said he, “this is a kittle passage! As long as we lie here we’re safe; but I’m nane sae muckle nearer to my ship or the coast of France. And as soon as we stand up and signal the brig, it’s another matter. For where will your gentry be, think ye?”
“Maybe they’re no come yet,” said I. “And even if they are, there’s one clear matter in our favour. They’ll be all arranged to take us, that’s true. But they’ll have arranged for our coming from the east, and here we are upon their west.”
“Ay,” says Alan, “I wish we were in some force, and this was a battle, we would have bonnily outmanoeuvred them! But it isnae, Davit; and the way it is, is a wee thing less inspiring to Alan Breck. I swither, Davie.”
“Time flies, Alan,” said I.
“I ken that,” said Alan. “I ken naething else, as the French folk say. But this is a dreidful case of heids or tails. O! if I could but ken where your gentry were!”
“Alan,” said I, “this is no like you. It’s got to be now or never.”

> “This is no me, quo’ he,”

sang Alan, with a queer face betwixt shame and drollery.

> “Neither you nor me, quo’ he, neither you nor me,

Wow, na, Johnnie man! neither you nor me.”

===Chinese===

可我记得，我们贴着地皮往那一片高高洼洼里爬，四面睃着亮眼睛，心口在肋骨底下擂鼓，那太阳和海面亮晃晃，风在硬草里沙沙响，兔子一只只往洞里栽，鸥鸟一阵阵往天上惊——那荒滩在我看来竟像个活物。无疑，若秘密守得住，这地方方方面面都是秘密上船的首选；即使如今事泄、有人盯守，我们还是能神不知鬼不觉爬到沙丘前头，从那儿俯瞰脚下的海滩和大海。

可到了这里，艾伦站住了。

「戴维，」他说，「这可是个烫手的关头！咱俩趴在这儿是安全的；可俺离俺的船、离法国海岸，一寸也没近。咱俩一站起来给双桅船打信号，就是另一码事了。你那位贵人大爷们，你说这会儿在哪儿？」

「兴许还没到，」我说，「就算到了，有一桩咱们占理：他们摆好了阵候咱们，不假；可他们摆的是候咱们打东边来，咱们偏偏在他们西边。」

「唉，」艾伦说，「要是咱们人手齐些、这一仗是打仗，那他们叫咱们兜得漂亮！可这不算打仗，大卫；眼下这局面，给艾伦·布雷克壮不起胆。俺犯嘀咕，戴维。」

「时间不等人，艾伦。」我说。

「俺知道，」艾伦说，「俺别的不知，就知道这个——法国人这么讲。可这一回是掷铜板，正反都是命。唉！俺要是知道你那几位大爷在哪儿就好了！」

「艾伦，」我说，「这可不像你了。事到如今，不成便休。」

> 「这不像俺，他说，」

艾伦唱起来，脸上又臊又逗，神情古怪。

> 「不像你也不像俺，他说，不像你也不像俺，

哇，不，约翰伙计！不像你也不像俺。」

===Original===

And then of a sudden he stood straight up where he was, and with a handkerchief flying in his right hand, marched down upon the beach. I stood up myself, but lingered behind him, scanning the sandhills to the east. His appearance was at first unremarked: Scougal not expecting him so early, and *my gentry* watching on the other side. Then they awoke on board the *Thistle*, and it seemed they had all in readiness, for there was scarce a second’s bustle on the deck before we saw a skiff put round her stern and begin to pull lively for the coast. Almost at the same moment of time, and perhaps half a mile away towards Gillane Ness, the figure of a man appeared for a blink upon a sandhill, waving with his arms; and though he was gone again in the same flash, the gulls in that part continued a little longer to fly wild.
Alan had not seen this, looking straight to seaward at the ship and skiff.
“It maun be as it will!” said he, when I had told him. “Weel may yon boatie row, or my craig’ll have to thole a raxing.”
That part of the beach was long and flat, and excellent walking when the tide was down; a little cressy burn flowed over it in one place to the sea; and the sandhills ran along the head of it like the rampart of a town. No eye of ours could spy what was passing behind there in the bents, no hurry of ours could mend the speed of the boat’s coming: time stood still with us through that uncanny period of waiting.
“There is one thing I would like to ken,” says Alan. “I would like fine to ken these gentry’s orders. We’re worth four hunner pound the pair of us: how if they took the guns to us, Davie? They would get a bonny shot from the top of that lang sandy bank.”
“Morally impossible,” said I. “The point is that they can have no guns. This thing has been gone about too secret; pistols they may have, but never guns.”
“I believe ye’ll be in the right,” says Alan. “For all which I am wearying a good deal for yon boat.”
And he snapped his fingers and whistled to it like a dog.
It was now perhaps a third of the way in, and we ourselves already hard on the margin of the sea, so that the soft sand rose over my shoes.

===Chinese===

唱罢，他忽然就地直挺挺站起来，右手挥着一方手帕，朝海滩大步走去。我也站起身，却缀在他身后，眼睛扫着东面的沙丘。起初没人留意他：斯库格尔没料着他来得这么早，*我那几位贵人*又在另一边守望。随后「蓟号」上的人惊觉了——看来他们早有准备，甲板上没忙乱两秒钟，一只小艇已绕过船尾，使劲朝岸边划来。几乎同时，往吉兰角方向约莫半英里处，一个人的身影在沙丘上一闪，挥舞着手臂；虽然一晃又没了，那一带的鸥鸟还是多受惊乱飞了一阵。

艾伦没看见这个，他正对着大海望船和艇。

「听天由命吧！」我把所见告诉他后，他说。「那小艇可得划快点，不然俺这脖子要受拉伸之刑喽。」

那段海滩又长又平，退潮后走起来极好；一道长着水芹的小溪在一处漫过滩面入海；沙丘沿着滩头一路排开，像城墙。我们的眼睛窥不透沙丘后硬草丛里的动静，我们的着急也加不了小艇一桨：在那段邪门的等待里，时间同我们一起站住了。

「有一样俺想弄明白，」艾伦说，「俺顶想弄明白这几位大爷领的是什么令。咱们两个值四百镑呢：他们要是冲咱们开枪怎么办，戴维？从那道长沙冈顶上放，可是个好枪位。」

「万万不会，」我说，「要紧的是他们不会有枪。这事办得太机密；手枪兴许有，火枪断不会有。」

「俺想你是对的呢，」艾伦说，「虽这么说，俺还是眼巴巴盼着那小艇。」

他打起了响指，又像唤狗似的朝它吹口哨。

小艇约莫划进了三分之一，我们自己已经贴近海水的边线，松软的沙子没过了我的鞋面。

===Original===

There was no more to do whatever but to wait, to look as much as we were able at the creeping nearer of the boat, and as little as we could manage at the long impenetrable front of the sandhills, over which the gulls twinkled and behind which our enemies were doubtless marshalling.
“This is a fine, bright, caller place to get shot in,” says Alan, suddenly; “and, man, I wish that I had your courage!”
“Alan!” I cried, “what kind of talk is this of it? You’re just made of courage; it’s the character of the man, as I could prove myself if there was nobody else.”
“And you would be the more mistaken,” said he. “What makes the differ with me is just my great penetration and knowledge of affairs. But for auld, cauld, dour, deidly courage, I am not fit to hold a candle to yourself. Look at us two here upon the sands. Here am I, fair hotching to be off; here’s you (for all that I ken) in two minds of it whether you’ll no stop. Do you think that I could do that, or would? No me! Firstly, because I havenae got the courage and wouldnae daur; and secondly, because I am a man of so much penetration and would see ye damned first.”
“It’s there ye’re coming, is it?” I cried. “Ah, man Alan, you can wile your old wives, but you never can wile me.”
Remembrance of my temptation in the wood made me strong as iron.
“I have a tryst to keep,” I continued. “I am trysted with your cousin Charlie; I have passed my word.”
“Braw trysts that you’ll can keep,” said Alan. “Ye’ll just mistryst aince and for a’ with the gentry in the bents. And what for?” he went on with an extreme threatening gravity. “Just tell me that, my mannie! Are ye to be speerited away like Lady Grange? Are they to drive a dirk in your inside and bury ye in the bents? Or is it to be the other way, and are they to bring ye in with James? Are they folk to be trustit? Would ye stick your head in the mouth of Sim Fraser and the ither Whigs?” he added with extraordinary bitterness.
“Alan,” cried I, “they’re all rogues and liars, and I’m with ye there. The more reason there should be one decent man in such a land of thieves! My word is passed, and I’ll stick to it. I said long syne to your kinswoman that I would stumble at no risk. Do ye mind of that?﻿—the night Red Colin fell, it was. No more I will, then. Here I stop. Prestongrange promised me my life; if he’s to be mansworn, here I’ll have to die.”

===Chinese===

除了等待，无事可做：能看就多看一眼那小艇一寸寸逼近，能少看就少看一眼沙丘那道深不可测的长脸——鸥鸟在丘顶上闪烁，我们敌人无疑正在丘后集结。

「挨枪子儿倒是个敞亮的好地方，」艾伦忽然说，「伙计，俺要有你那胆量就好了！」

「艾伦！」我叫道，「你这是什么话！你整个人就是胆量造的；这是你这人的品性，不用旁人证明，我自己就能证明。」

「那你可就错上加错了，」他说，「俺同你的分别，全在俺的洞察力大、阅历深。至于那种老派的、冷硬的、不要命的胆量，俺给你提鞋都不配。瞧瞧咱俩在这沙滩上：俺这儿急得直蹦，恨不得立时走人；你呢（就俺所知），还在盘算要不要留下。你以为俺办得到吗？俺才不呢！第一，俺没那个胆，不敢；第二，俺洞察力太强，宁肯看你见鬼去。」

「绕来绕去你就等在这儿，是不是？」我叫道，「唉，艾伦啊，你哄得了你那些老婆子，哄不了我。」

想起树林里那回的动摇，此刻我硬得像铁。

「我有个约会要赴，」我接着说，「我同你表哥查理约好了的；我话已出口。」

「好一个赴得成的约，」艾伦说，「你这就要同草丛里那几位大爷一了百了地『误』上一约了。图什么呢？」他板起脸，威吓到十二分。「你就说说，小家伙！你是要像格兰奇夫人那样被人掳走？是要叫人一刀捅进肚子、埋进沙草？还是倒过来，要把你送去同詹姆斯作伴？那号人配信吗？你肯把脑袋伸进西姆·弗雷泽那帮辉格党嘴里去？」他加了一句，怨毒无比。

「艾伦，」我叫道，「他们都是流氓和骗子，这个我同你一条心。可正因为这样，这片贼窝里更该有一个体面人！我话已出口，就作数。早先我对你那位表亲说过：什么风险我都不却步。你记不记得？——就是红科林倒下那晚。如今也一样。我就站在这儿。普雷斯顿格兰奇许过我性命；他要是毁诺，我就死在这儿。」

===Original===

“Aweel, aweel,” said Alan.
All this time we had seen or heard no more of our pursuers. In truth we had caught them unawares; their whole party (as I was to learn afterwards) had not yet reached the scene; what there was of them was spread among the bents towards Gillane. It was quite an affair to call them in and bring them over, and the boat was making speed. They were besides but cowardly fellows: a mere leash of Highland cattle thieves, of several clans, no gentleman there to be the captain: and the more they looked at Alan and me upon the beach, the less (I must suppose) they liked the looks of us.
Whoever had betrayed Alan it was not the captain: he was in the skiff himself, steering and stirring up his oarsmen, like a man with his heart in his employ. Already he was near in, and the boat scouring﻿—already Alan’s face had flamed crimson with the excitement of his deliverance, when our friends in the bents, either in despair to see their prey escape them or with some hope of scaring Andie, raised suddenly a shrill cry of several voices.
This sound, arising from what appeared to be a quite deserted coast, was really very daunting, and the men in the boat held water instantly.
“What’s this of it?” sings out the captain, for he was come within an easy hail.
“Freens o’ mine,” says Alan, and began immediately to wade forth in the shallow water towards the boat. “Davie,” he said, pausing, “Davie, are ye no coming? I am swier to leave ye.”
“Not a hair of me,” said I.
He stood part of a second where he was to his knees in the salt water, hesitating.
“He that will to Cupar, maun to Cupar,” said he, and swashing in deeper than his waist, was hauled into the skiff, which was immediately directed for the ship.
I stood where he had left me, with my hands behind my back; Alan sat with his head turned watching me; and the boat drew smoothly away. Of a sudden I came the nearest hand to shedding tears, and seemed to myself the most deserted, solitary lad in Scotland. With that I turned my back upon the sea and faced the sand hills. There was no sight or sound of man; the sun shone on the wet sand and the dry, the wind blew in the bents, the gulls made a dreary piping. As I passed higher up the beach, the sand-lice were hopping nimbly about the stranded tangles. The devil any other sight or sound in that unchancy place.

===Chinese===

「也罢，也罢。」艾伦说。

这半天，追兵再没露面也没出声。其实是我们打了个措手不及：他们一伙（这是我后来得知的）还没到齐；到了的都散在吉兰方向的草丛里。把他们召齐、调过来颇费周章，而小艇越划越近。再说他们本来就是些胆小鬼：不过是几个高地偷牛贼，杂凑的几个氏族，没有一个绅士做头；他们在丘后瞧着艾伦同我站在滩上，越瞧（我敢说）越不爱我们这副长相。

出卖艾伦的人是谁，反正不是船长：他亲自坐在小艇里掌舵，给桨手们打气，一副心思全在差事上的人。他已经快到岸边，小艇破浪而来——艾伦的脸已经因脱身在望烧得通红——不料我们草丛里的朋友们，或是眼见猎物要跑急了眼，或是想唬一唬安迪，突然齐声发出一声尖啸。

这声音从看似绝无人迹的海岸上升起，实在瘆人，艇上的人立刻刹住了桨。

「这是怎么回事？」船长扬声问，他已到了一喊就应的距离。

「俺的朋友们，」艾伦说，随即趟进浅水朝小艇走去。「戴维，」他停了停，说，「戴维，你不来吗？丢下你，俺不落忍。」

「我一根头发也不会动。」我说。

他在原地站了一瞬，齐膝立在咸水里，犹豫着。

「该去库帕的，就得去库帕。」他说，哗哗蹚到深过腰际的地方，被人拽上小艇，艇头随即调向大船。

我站在他留下我的地方，两手背在身后；艾伦坐着，回过头望我；小艇平平地滑远了。忽然间，我差一点落泪，觉得自己是全苏格兰最孤苦、最没人管的少年。我转身背海，面向沙丘。没有人影，没有人声；太阳照着湿沙和干沙，风吹着硬草，鸥鸟叫声凄凉。我沿滩往上走，沙虱在搁浅的海草间灵活地蹦跳。这邪门地方，鬼也没有一个。

===Original===

And yet I knew there were folk there, observing me, upon some secret purpose. They were no soldiers, or they would have fallen on and taken us ere now; doubtless they were some common rogues hired for my undoing, perhaps to kidnap, perhaps to murder me outright. From the position of those engaged, the first was the more likely; from what I knew of their character and ardency in this business, I thought the second very possible; and the blood ran cold about my heart.
I had a mad idea to loosen my sword in the scabbard; for though I was very unfit to stand up like a gentleman blade to blade, I thought I could do some scathe in a random combat. But I perceived in time the folly of resistance. This was no doubt the joint “expedient” on which Prestongrange and Fraser were agreed. The first, I was very sure, had done something to secure my life; the second was pretty likely to have slipped in some contrary hints into the ears of Neil and his companions; and if I were to show bare steel I might play straight into the hands of my worst enemy and seal my own doom.
These thoughts brought me to the head of the beach. I cast a look behind, the boat was nearing the brig, and Alan flew his handkerchief for a farewell, which I replied to with the waving of my hand. But Alan himself was shrunk to a small thing in my view, alongside of this pass that lay in front of me. I set my hat hard on my head, clenched my teeth, and went right before me up the face of the sand-wreath. It made a hard climb, being steep, and the sand like water underfoot. But I caught hold at last by the long bent grass on the brae-top, and pulled myself to a good footing. The same moment men stirred and stood up here and there, six or seven of them, ragged-like knaves, each with a dagger in his hand. The fair truth is, I shut my eyes and prayed. When I opened them again, the rogues were crept the least thing nearer without speech or hurry. Every eye was upon mine, which struck me with a strange sensation of their brightness, and of the fear with which they continued to approach me. I held out my hands empty: whereupon one asked, with a strong Highland brogue, if I surrendered.
“Under protest,” said I, “if ye ken what that means, which I misdoubt.”

===Chinese===

然而我知道那里有人，正为着什么秘密目的监视我。他们不是兵，不然早扑上来把我们拿了；无疑是一伙收钱坏我事的寻常歹徒，或为绑票，或干脆要我命。从这伙人的布置看，前者更像；从我所知的他们在此事上的心性热肠看，后者大有可能；我心底的血凉了一圈。

我起过一个疯狂的念头：把剑在鞘里松一松；虽然要我像绅士那样拔剑对刺，我全不够格，乱战一场总能带倒几个。可我及时看出了抵抗的愚蠢。这无疑是普雷斯顿格兰奇同弗雷泽说定的那个联合「权宜之计」。头一个，我十分确信，出过力保我的性命；第二个，很可能往尼尔和他同伴们耳朵里灌过相反的话；我若亮出刀剑，正好把刀柄递到我最凶的仇人手里，自己签字画押。

这些念头把我带到了滩头。我回头一望：小艇已挨近双桅船，艾伦挥着手帕道别，我也挥手作答。可在我眼里，艾伦本人已经缩得很小了——比起横在我面前的这道关。我把帽子往头上一扣，咬紧牙关，径直往沙冈的坡面上走。那一段爬得吃力：坡陡，脚下的沙子又像水。可我总算抓住了坡顶的长硬草，把自己拽了上去。同一瞬间，这里那里有人动了动、站了起来，六七个，都是些褴褛的汉子，人人手里一把匕首。说老实话，我闭上眼睛做了祷告。再睁开时，那伙流氓已不出声、不慌不忙地又逼近了一点点。所有的眼睛都盯着我的眼睛；那眼神的亮，他们一步步逼近时那份小心翼翼，给了我一种奇异的感觉。我摊开两手，示意空空；于是有一个操着浓重高地土腔的问：我投不投降。

「带保留地投降，」我说，「这话你们懂不懂，我很怀疑。」

===Original===

At that word, they came all in upon me like a flight of birds upon a carrion, seized me, took my sword, and all the money from my pockets, bound me hand and foot with some strong line, and cast me on a tussock of bent. There they sat about their captive in a part of a circle and gazed upon him silently like something dangerous, perhaps a lion or a tiger on the spring. Presently this attention was relaxed. They drew nearer together, fell to speech in the Gaelic, and very cynically divided my property before my eyes. It was my diversion in this time that I could watch from my place the progress of my friend’s escape. I saw the boat come to the brig and be hoisted in, the sails fill, and the ship pass out seaward behind the isles and by North Berwick.
In the course of two hours or so, more and more ragged Highlandmen kept collecting, Neil among the first, until the party must have numbered near a score. With each new arrival there was a fresh bout of talk, that sounded like complaints and explanations; but I observed one thing, none of those that came late had any share in the division of my spoils. The last discussion was very violent and eager, so that once I thought they would have quarrelled; on the heels of which their company parted, the bulk of them returning westward in a troop, and only three, Neil and two others, remaining sentries on the prisoner.
“I could name one who would be very ill pleased with your day’s work, Neil Duncanson,” said I, when the rest had moved away.
He assured me in answer I should be tenderly used, for he knew he was “acquent wi’ the leddy.”
This was all our talk, nor did any other son of man appear upon that portion of the coast until the sun had gone down among the Highland mountains, and the gloaming was beginning to grow dark. At which hour I was aware of a long, lean, bony-like Lothian man of a very swarthy countenance, that came towards us among the bents on a farm horse.
“Lads,” cried he, “hae ye a paper like this?” and held up one in his hand. Neil produced a second, which the newcomer studied through a pair of horn spectacles, and saying all was right and we were the folk he was seeking, immediately dismounted. I was then set in his place, my feet tied under the horse’s belly, and we set forth under the guidance of the Lowlander.

===Chinese===

话音一落，他们一齐扑上来，像一群鸟扑一具腐尸，抓住我，下了我的剑，掏空我口袋里所有的钱，用结实的绳索捆了手脚，把我撂在一丛硬草墩上。他们围着俘虏坐成半个圈，默不作声地盯着他，像盯一样危险的东西——一头就要扑出的狮子或老虎。不久这份专注松懈了，他们凑作一堆，用盖尔语交谈，当着我的面把我的财物极不客气地分了。这段时间里我的消遣，是从我的位置上瞭望朋友脱险的进程：我看着小艇靠上双桅船、被吊上船去，帆吃饱了风，船绕过诸岛、过北贝里克，出海去了。

约莫两个钟头里，褴褛的高地人越聚越多，尼尔是头一批到的，最后总数近二十。每来一批就有一轮新的交谈，听着像抱怨和辩解；可我注意到一件事：来迟的，分我东西时一个子儿也没份。最后一场争论激烈非常，有一度我以为他们要内讧；争完，人马散伙，大股结队往西去了，只留三个——尼尔和另外两个——看守俘虏。

「有一个人对你们今天的活计不会高兴的，尼尔·邓肯森，我可以说出他是谁。」等旁人走远了，我说。

他答话说，我会受优待的，因为他「同那位小姐有交情」。

这是我们全部的交谈；此后再无一个人影在那段海岸上出现，直到太阳落进高地的群山、暮色渐浓。这时分，我看见草丛间有一个又高又瘦、骨头嶙峋的洛锡安人骑着一匹农家马过来，面色黧黑。

「伙计们，」他叫道，「你们可有这样一张字据？」手里举着一张。尼尔掏出另一张，来人隔着角质眼镜端详一番，说都对、正是他要找的人，随即下了马。于是我给安置在马背上，两脚捆在马肚皮下，由这个低地人领路出发了。

===Original===

His path must have been very well chosen, for we met but one pair﻿—a pair of lovers﻿—the whole way, and these, perhaps taking us to be free-traders, fled on our approach. We were at one time close at the foot of Berwick Law on the south side; at another, as we passed over some open hills, I spied the lights of a clachan and the old tower of a church among some trees not far off, but too far to cry for help, if I had dreamed of it. At last we came again within sound of the sea. There was moonlight, though not much; and by this I could see the three huge towers and broken battlements of Tantallon, that old chief place of the Red Douglases. The horse was picketed in the bottom of the ditch to graze, and I was led within, and forth into the court, and thence into a tumbledown stone hall. Here my conductors built a brisk fire in the midst of the pavement, for there was a chill in the night. My hands were loosed, I was set by the wall in the inner end, and (the Lowlander having produced provisions) I was given oatmeal bread and a pitcher of French brandy. This done, I was left once more alone with my three Highlandmen. They sat close by the fire drinking and talking; the wind blew in by the breaches, cast about the smoke and flames, and sang in the tops of the towers; I could hear the sea under the cliffs, and my mind being reassured as to my life, and my body and spirits wearied with the day’s employment, I turned upon one side and slumbered.
I had no means of guessing at what hour I was wakened, only the moon was down and the fire low. My feet were now loosed, and I was carried through the ruins and down the cliff-side by a precipitous path to where I found a fisher’s boat in a haven of the rocks. This I was had on board of, and we began to put forth from the shore in a fine starlight.

===Chinese===

他选的路必是挑得极好：一路我们只遇上一对——一对情侣——也许把我们当成了走私贩子，见我们过来撒腿就跑。有一阵我们贴着贝里克劳山南坡脚下走；又一阵翻过几面秃丘，我望见一个小村落的灯火和树丛里一座旧教堂的塔，可惜太远，喊救命够不着——倘若我起过这念头的话。末了，我们又回到了听得见海声的地方。有月光，虽不很亮；借着它，我认出坦塔龙的三大座塔楼和残破的城垛——红道格拉斯家那座老巢。马拴在壕沟底吃草，我被领进寨门，穿过院子，进了一间半塌的石厅。押送的人在地当间儿生起一堆旺火，夜里有些寒气。我的手松了绑，被安置在里头靠墙坐着；（低地人掏出吃食）给了我燕麦饼和一罐法国白兰地。完事之后，我又同我那三个高地人留在一处。他们围着火堆坐着喝酒说话；风从破口里灌进来，搅着烟和火苗，在塔顶上呼啸；我听得见悬崖底下的海。性命既已无忧，身子和精神又累乏了一整天，我侧过身，睡着了。

我无从猜自己是几时被叫醒的，只知道月亮落了，火也塌了。脚上的绑松了，他们架着我穿过废墟，沿一条陡径下了崖壁，到一处岩间的小港湾，那里泊着一条渔船。我被弄上船，趁着满天好星光，离了岸。

## XXII Helvoetsluys / 二十二、赫尔沃特斯路易斯
===Original===

The weather in the end considerably worsened; the wind sang in the shrouds, the sea swelled higher, and the ship began to labour and cry out among the billows. The song of the leadsman in the chains was now scarce ceasing, for we thrid all the way among shoals. About nine in the morning, in a burst of wintry sun between two squalls of hail, I had my first look of Holland﻿—a line of windmills birling in the breeze. It was besides my first knowledge of these daft-like contrivances, which gave me a near sense of foreign travel and a new world and life. We came to an anchor about half-past eleven, outside the harbour of Helvoetsluys, in a place where the sea sometimes broke and the ship pitched outrageously. You may be sure we were all on deck save Mrs. Gebbie, some of us in cloaks, others mantled in the ship’s tarpaulins, all clinging on by ropes, and jesting the most like old sailor-folk that we could imitate.
Presently a boat, that was backed like a partan-crab, came gingerly alongside, and the skipper of it hailed our master in the Dutch. Thence Captain Sang turned, very troubled like, to Catriona; and the rest of us crowding about, the nature of the difficulty was made plain to all. The *Rose* was bound to the port of Rotterdam, whither the other passengers were in a great impatience to arrive, in view of a conveyance due to leave that very evening in the direction of the Upper Germany. This, with the present half-gale of wind, the captain (if no time were lost) declared himself still capable to save. Now James More had trysted in Helvoet with his daughter, and the captain had engaged to call before the port and place her (according to the custom) in a shore boat. There was the boat, to be sure, and there was Catriona ready: but both our master and the patroon of the boat scrupled at the risk, and the first was in no humour to delay.
“Your father,” said he, “would be gey an little pleased if we was to break a leg to ye, Miss Drummond, let-a-be drowning of you. Take my way of it,” says he, “and come on-by with the rest of us here to Rotterdam. Ye can get a passage down the Maes in a sailing scoot as far to the Brill, and thence on again, by a place in a rattel-wagon, back to Helvoet.”
But Catriona would hear of no change.

===Chinese===

天气到末了坏了许多；风在桅索里啸，海涌得更高，船在浪里开始吃力、开始喋喋叫唤。锚链台上测水手那支歌这时几乎没有停过，因为我们一路都在浅滩之间穿行。早晨九点钟光景，两场雹子之间漏出一线冬日，我头一眼望见了荷兰——一排风车在风里滴溜溜地转。这也是我头一回见识这类痴痴傻傻的机巧玩意儿，给了我一种切近的出国感：一个新的世界、新的生活。十一点半，我们在赫尔沃特斯路易斯港外抛了锚；那地方海面时而翻起白浪，船颠得不成样子。可以想见，除了格比太太，我们全上了甲板：有的披斗篷，有的裹船上的油布，个个抓着缆绳，学着老水手的腔调，能多像就多像地说笑。
不多时，一条小艇小心翼翼靠拢来——那艇倒退着走，活像一只毛蟹——艇主用荷兰话向我们船长喊话。桑格舰长随即转过身来，满面愁容，对卡特琳娜说话；我们围拢上去，难处的底细便人人都清楚了。「玫瑰号」的目的港是鹿特丹；船上的乘客个个心急火燎地要赶到，因为有一班交通当晚就要启程，往上德意志去。趁着眼下这半场大风，舰长说（只要一刻不耽搁）还赶得及。偏偏詹姆斯·莫尔同他女儿约的相会地点是赫尔沃特，舰长又应承过船到港前停一停，把她（按惯例）交到一条上岸的小艇上。小艇是有了，卡特琳娜也齐整了；可是我们的船长和小艇的艇主都忌惮这个险，船长又一点不肯耽搁。
「要是给你折了一条腿，德拉蒙德小姐，」他说，「你父亲可不见得怎么高兴，何况是淹死你。依我说，随我们大家一路去鹿特丹。你可以搭一条帆船下马斯河到布里尔，再从那儿坐一辆邮车里的位子，回赫尔沃特来。」
可卡特琳娜说什么也不肯改。

===Original===

She looked white-like as she beheld the bursting of the sprays, the green seas that sometimes poured upon the forecastle, and the perpetual bounding and swooping of the boat among the billows; but she stood firmly by her father’s orders. “My father, James More, will have arranged it so,” was her first word and her last. I thought it very idle and indeed wanton in the girl to be so literal and stand opposite to so much kind advice; but the fact is she had a very good reason, if she would have told us. Sailing scoots and rattel-wagons are excellent things; only the use of them must first be paid for, and all she was possessed of in the world was just two shillings and a penny halfpenny sterling. So it fell out that captain and passengers, not knowing of her destitution﻿—and she being too proud to tell them﻿—spoke in vain.
“But you ken nae French and nae Dutch neither,” said one.
“It is very true,” says she, “but since the year ’46 there are so many of the honest Scots abroad that I will be doing very well, I thank you.”
There was a pretty country simplicity in this that made some laugh, others looked the more sorry, and Mr. Gebbie fall outright in a passion. I believe he knew it was his duty (his wife having accepted charge of the girl) to have gone ashore with her and seen her safe; nothing would have induced him to have done so, since it must have involved the loss of his conveyance; and I think he made it up to his conscience by the loudness of his voice. At least he broke out upon Captain Sang, raging and saying the thing was a disgrace; that it was mere death to try to leave the ship, and at any event we could not cast down an innocent maid in a boatful of nasty Holland fishers, and leave her to her fate. I was thinking something of the same; took the mate upon one side, arranged with him to send on my chests by track-scoot to an address I had in Leyden, and stood up and signalled to the fishers.
“I will go ashore with the young lady, Captain Sang,” said I. “It is all one what way I go to Leyden;” and leaped at the same time into the boat, which I managed not so elegantly but what I fell with two of the fishers in the bilge.
From the boat the business appeared yet more precarious than from the ship, she stood so high over us, swung down so swift, and menaced us so perpetually with her plunging and passaging upon the anchor cable.

===Chinese===

她望着炸开的浪花、不时泼上前甲板的绿水、小艇在浪里不住的腾跃和俯冲，脸色发白；可是她牢牢守着父亲的吩咐。「我父亲詹姆斯·莫尔，自有他这样安排的道理。」这是她的头一句话，也是最后一句。我心里觉得这姑娘死心眼得近乎任性，站在那里同这么多好意作对；其实呢，她有一个极好的理由，只是不肯告诉我们。帆船和邮车都是好东西，只是先得花钱；她在这世上全部的家当，拤共两先令外加一便士半。于是乎，船长和乘客们不知道她的穷——她又太骄傲，不肯说——全都说的是白话。
「可你既不懂法文，又不懂荷兰文。」有人说。
「正是，」她说，「可是自四六年以后，流落在外的老实苏格兰人这样多，我会对付得很好，谢谢你们。」
这话里那份乡土的天真，引得有的笑，有的愈发怜惜，格比先生却当场发了火。我相信他心里明白这是他的本分（他太太既已接下了照管这姑娘的担子），本该随她上岸、亲眼看她平安；可是再没有什么能叫他这样办，因为那就要误了他的班船；我想他是拿嗓门的大来跟他的良心结账。至少他冲着桑格舰长发作起来，咆哮着说这事简直丢脸；说这时候离船无异送死；又说无论如何，我们不能把一个清白的姑娘攋进一船腌臜的荷兰渔夫中间，就此不管她的死活。我心里想的也有几分相同；我把大副拉到一边，同他说好，把我的箱子由驳船运到我在莱顿的一个地址，随即站起来，朝渔船打手势。
「我陪这位小姐上岸，桑格舰长，」我说，「去莱顿走哪条道对我全是一样。」话音未落，人已跳进小艇——跳得实在不算体面，同两个渔夫一起栽进了舱底污水里。
从小艇上看，这事比在船上看还要悬：船在我们头顶上那样高，摆下来那样快，绕着锚缆前冲后撞，时时刻刻威脅着我们。

===Original===

I began to think I had made a fool’s bargain, that it was merely impossible Catriona should be got on board to me, and that I stood to be set ashore at Helvoet all by myself and with no hope of any reward but the pleasure of embracing James More, if I should want to. But this was to reckon without the lass’s courage. She had seen me leap with very little appearance (however much reality) of hesitation; to be sure, she was not to be beat by her discarded friend. Up she stood on the bulwarks and held by a stay, the wind blowing in her petticoats, which made the enterprise more dangerous and gave us rather more of a view of her stockings than would be thought genteel in cities. There was no minute lost, and scarce time given for any to interfere if they had wished the same. I stood up on the other side and spread my arms; the ship swung down on us, the patroon humoured his boat nearer in than was perhaps wholly safe, and Catriona leaped into the air. I was so happy as to catch her, and the fishers readily supporting us, escaped a fall. She held to me a moment very tight, breathing quick and deep; thence (she still clinging to me with both hands) we were passed aft to our places by the steersman; and Captain Sang and all the crew and passengers cheering and crying farewell, the boat was put about for shore.
As soon as Catriona came a little to herself she unhanded me suddenly but said no word. No more did I; and indeed the whistling of the wind and the breaching of the sprays made it no time for speech; and our crew not only toiled excessively but made extremely little way, so that the *Rose* had got her anchor and was off again before we had approached the harbour mouth.
We were no sooner in smooth water than the patroon, according to their beastly Hollands custom, stopped his boat and required of us our fares. Two guilders was the man’s demand, between three and four shillings English money, for each passenger. But at this Catriona began to cry out with a vast deal of agitation. She had asked of Captain Sang, she said, and the fare was but an English shilling. “Do you think I will have come on board and not ask first?” cries she.

===Chinese===

我开始想，我这是做了一笔傻瓜的买卖：卡特琳娜根本没法弄上艇来；我就要一个人被攋在赫尔沃特，指望不到别的报偿，只有——要是我愿意的话——拥抱詹姆斯·莫尔的那点乐趣。可这样算，就没算上那姑娘的胆量。她瞧见我跳了下来，犹豫（不管心里翻不翻腾）几乎没有半分；当然，她不能叫这位被她撇下的朋友比下去。她站上舷墙，攀住一根支索，风灌进她的裙子，把这一番壮举弄得更加危险，也叫我们把她那双长袜多看了几眼——在城里人看来未免有伤大雅。一刻也没有耽搁，谁想拦也来不及。我在另一边站起来，张开两臂；船朝我们摆下来，艇主把艇又往里靠了靠——多半不十分稳当——卡特琳娜向空中一跃。我有幸接住了她；渔夫们一把扶住我们，免了摔。她把我紧紧抱了片刻，呼吸又急又深；随后（她两手仍抓着我不放）由舵手把我们送到后梢各自的位子上；桑格舰长同全船的水手乘客一齐欢呼道别，小艇掉头向岸。
卡特琳娜一缓过神来，忽然撒开手，却一句话不说。我也不说；实在因为风啸浪吼，原不是说话的时候；我们那班船夫不但划得死去活来，艇却走得极慢，「玫瑰号」起了锚又开船了，我们还没拤近港口的口子。
一进平水，艇主便按他们那套荷兰人的混账规矩，把艇一停，讨我们的船钱。一个人两个荷兰盾，合英国钱三先令到四先令。卡特琳娜一听就急得嚷起来。她说，她问过桑格舰长，船钱不过一个英国先令。「你们当我不先问一声就上船么？」她嚷道。

===Original===

The patroon scolded back upon her in a lingo where the oaths were English and the rest right Hollands; till at last (seeing her near tears) I privately slipped in the rogue’s hand six shillings, whereupon he was obliging enough to receive from her the other shilling without more complaint. No doubt I was a good deal nettled and ashamed. I like to see folk thrifty but not with so much passion; and I daresay it would be rather coldly that I asked her, as the boat moved on again for shore, where it was that she was trysted with her father.
“He is to be inquired of at the house of one Sprott, an honest Scotch merchant,” says she; and then with the same breath, “I am wishing to thank you very much﻿—you are a brave friend to me.”
“It will be time enough when I get you to your father,” said I, little thinking that I spoke so true. “I can tell him a fine tale of a loyal daughter.”
“O, I do not think I will be a loyal girl, at all events,” she cried, with a great deal of painfulness in the expression. “I do not think my heart is true.”
“Yet there are very few that would have made that leap, and all to obey a father’s orders,” I observed.
“I cannot have you to be thinking of me so,” she cried again. “When you had done that same, how would I stop behind? And at all events that was not all the reasons.” Whereupon, with a burning face, she told me the plain truth upon her poverty.
“Good guide us!” cried I, “what kind of daft-like proceeding is this, to let yourself be launched on the continent of Europe with an empty purse﻿—I count it hardly decent﻿—scant decent!” I cried.
“You forget James More, my father, is a poor gentleman,” said she. “He is a hunted exile.”
“But I think not all your friends are hunted exiles,” I exclaimed. “And was this fair to them that care for you? Was it fair to me? was it fair to Miss Grant that counselled you to go, and would be driven fair horn-mad if she could hear of it? Was it even fair to these Gregory folk that you were living with, and used you lovingly? It’s a blessing you have fallen in my hands! Suppose your father hindered by an accident, what would become of you here, and you your lee-alone in a strange place? The thought of the thing frightens me,” I said.
“I will have lied to all of them,” she replied. “I will have told them all that I had plenty. I told *her* too. I could not be lowering James More to them.”

===Chinese===

艇主用一种咒骂是英国话、其余全是荷兰话的语言回骂她；末了（见她要哭）我偷偷往那无赖手里塞了六先令，他才肯客客气气收下她那一先令，不再抱怨。不用说，我又恼又臊。我爱见人节俭，可不爱见这么大的火气；小艇重新往岸边走时，我问她同父亲约定在哪里相会，口气多半是冷冷的。
「到一个叫斯普罗特的人家里去打听，一位老实的苏格兰商人。」她说；接着一口气又道，「我要好好谢你——你待我真够朋友。」
「等你到了你父亲手里再谢不迟。」我说，哪想到这句话说得那样应景。「我能给他讲一个孝顺女儿的好故事。」
「哦，我看我算不得孝顺，怎么也算不得。」她喊道，神情里满是苦处。「我看我的心不真。」
「可是肯那样一跳的人没有几个，还全是为着遵父命。」我说。
「别这样想我，」她又喊道，「你既跳了，我怎么能落在后头？再说，那也不全是缘故。」说着她满脸通红，把她的穷困一五一十对我和盘托出。
「老天爷！」我喊道，「这是怎样一桩傻事——兜里空空就敢往欧罗巴大陆上跑——我说这算不得体面——太不体面了！」
「你忘了詹姆斯·莫尔我父亲是个穷绅士，」她说，「他是个被缉捕的流亡人。」
「可你的朋友总不见得个个是被缉捕的流亡人吧，」我喊道，「你这样对得起那些疼你的人么？对得起我么？对得起劝你走的格兰特小姐么——她要是听见，准得气个半死？就连对你住的那家格雷戈里，人家疼疼热热地待你——你对得起他们么？亏得你落在我手里！你父亲万一叫什么事绊住了，你一个人、只身一个、人生地不熟，可怎么办？想一想我就害怕。」我说。
「我对他们全都说了瞎话，」她答道，「我对他们全说我有的是钱。对她也说了。我不能叫詹姆斯·莫尔在他们跟前矮下去。」

===Original===

I found out later on that she must have lowered him in the very dust, for the lie was originally the father’s not the daughter’s, and she thus obliged to persevere in it for the man’s reputation. But at the time I was ignorant of this, and the mere thought of her destitution and the perils in which she must have fallen, had ruffled me almost beyond reason.
“Well, well, well,” said I, “you will have to learn more sense.”
I left her mails for the moment in an inn upon the shore, where I got a direction for Sprott’s house in my new French, and we walked there﻿—it was some little way﻿—beholding the place with wonder as we went. Indeed, there was much for Scots folk to admire; canals and trees being intermingled with the houses; the houses, each within itself, of a brave red brick, the colour of a rose, with steps and benches of blue marble at the cheek of every door, and the whole town so clean you might have dined upon the causeway. Sprott was within, upon his ledgers, in a low parlour, very neat and clean, and set out with china and pictures and a globe of the earth in a brass frame. He was a big-chafted, ruddy, lusty man, with a crooked hard look to him; and he made us not that much civility as offer us a seat.
“Is James More Macgregor now in Helvoet, sir?” says I.
“I ken nobody by such a name,” says he, impatient-like.
“Since you are so particular,” says I, “I will amend my question, and ask you where we are to find in Helvoet one James Drummond, *alias* Macgregor, *alias* James More, late tenant in Iveronachile?”
“Sir,” says he, “he may be in Hell for what I ken, and for my part I wish he was.”
“The young lady is that gentleman’s daughter, sir,” said I, “before whom, I think you will agree with me, it is not very becoming to discuss his character.”
“I have nothing to make either with him, or her, or you!” cries he in his gross voice.
“Under your favour, Mr. Sprott,” said I, “this young lady is come from Scotland seeking him, and by whatever mistake, was given the name of your house for a direction. An error it seems to have been, but I think this places both you and me﻿—who am but her fellow-traveller by accident﻿—under a strong obligation to help our countrywoman.”
“Will you ding me daft?” he cries. “I tell ye I ken naething and care less either for him or his breed. I tell ye the man owes me money.”

===Chinese===

后来我才打听明白，只怕他早已矮到了泥里：那瞎话起头本是那位父亲的，不是女儿的；她为着那人的名声，只好一条道走到黑。可当时我并不知道；单想她的穷、想她可能陷入的重重险境，我的火就几乎压不住。
「行了，行了，」我说，「你还得学着多懂点事。」
我把她的行李暂且撂在岸上一家客栈，在那里用我新学的法文问到去斯普罗特家的路；我们步行过去——有不短的一段路——一路看着那地方，啧啧称奇。真的，苏格兰人可看可叹的太多了：运河和树木同房屋错杂在一起；房屋一座座齐齐整整，上好的红砖，玫瑰的颜色，每扇门旁边是蓝色大理石的台阶和长凳；全城干净得能在街心石板上摆饭桌。斯普罗特正在家里对着账本，坐在一间低矮的客厅里，十分整洁，摆着瓷器、图画，还有一只铜框的地球仪。他是个宽胸膛、红脸膛、壮壮实实的汉子，眼里带着一种又硬又斜的神气；他给我们的礼数，连让个座也没有。
「请问詹姆斯·莫尔·麦克格雷戈先生如今在赫尔沃特么，先生？」我说。
「我不认得这么个名字。」他不耐烦地说。
「您既然这样讲究，」我说，「我就把话改一改：我们到赫尔沃特哪里，能找到一位詹姆斯·德鲁蒙德，化名麦克格雷戈，化名詹姆斯·莫尔，从前伊弗罗纳基尔的佃户？」
「先生，」他说，「他就是在地狱里也不干我事；要依我说，但愿他真在。」
「这位小姐就是那位先生的女儿，先生，」我说，「我想您会同我一致：当着她的面议论那位先生的品行，不大合适吧。」
「我同他、同她、同你，一概没什么可做的！」他扯着粗嗓门喊。
「恕我直言，斯普罗特先生，」我说，「这位小姐从苏格兰来找他，也不知怎么阴差阳错，拿您的宅子当了寻访的地址。看去是一场错；可我想，这一来您同我——我不过是个偶然同路的旅伴——都担了一份不小的义务，得帮衬我们的女同胞。」
「你要把我说疯么？」他喊道，「我告诉你，他的事我一概不知，也一概不问。我告诉你，那人欠我的钱。」

===Original===

“That may very well be, sir,” said I, who was now rather more angry than himself. “At least I owe you nothing; the young lady is under my protection; and I am neither at all used with these manners, nor in the least content with them.”
As I said this, and without particularly thinking what I did, I drew a step or two nearer to his table; thus striking, by mere good fortune, on the only argument that could at all affect the man. The blood left his lusty countenance.
“For the Lord’s sake dinna be hasty, sir!” he cried. “I am truly wishfu’ no to be offensive. But ye ken, sir, I’m like a wheen guid-natured, honest, canty auld fallows﻿—my bark is waur nor my bite. To hear me, ye micht whiles fancy I was a wee thing dour; but na, na! its a kind auld fellow at heart, Sandie Sprott! And ye could never imagine the fyke and fash this man has been to me.”
“Very good, sir,” said I. “Then I will make that much freedom with your kindness, as trouble you for your last news of Mr. Drummond.”
“You’re welcome, sir!” said he. “As for the young leddy (my respec’s to her!) he’ll just have clean forgotten her. I ken the man, ye see; I have lost siller by him ere now. He thinks of naebody but just himsel’; clan, king, or dauchter, if he can get his wameful, he would give them a’ the go-by! ay, or his correspondent either. For there is a sense in whilk I may be nearly almost said to be his correspondent. The fact is, we are employed thegether in a business affair, and I think it’s like to turn out a dear affair for Sandie Sprott. The man’s as guid’s my pairtner, and I give ye my mere word I ken naething by where he is. He micht be coming here to Helvoet; he micht come here the morn, he michtnae come for a twalmonth; I would wonder at naething﻿—or just at the ae thing, and that’s if he was to pay me my siller. Ye see what way I stand with it; and it’s clear I’m no very likely to meddle up with the young leddy, as ye ca’ her. She cannae stop here, that’s ae thing certain sure. Dod, sir, I’m a lone man! If I was to tak her in, its highly possible the hellicat would try and gar me marry her when he turned up.”
“Enough of this talk,” said I. “I will take the young lady among better friends. Give me pen, ink, and paper, and I will leave here for James More the address of my correspondent in Leyden. He can inquire from me where he is to seek his daughter.”

===Chinese===

「那很可能，先生，」我说，如今火气倒比他还大了。「至少我不欠您什么；这位小姐在我保护之下；这样的做派，我一向不惯，也一点不喜欢。」
我说这话时，并没有多想自己在做什么，朝他的桌子拤近了一两步；这一下纯属碰巧，恰好打中了唯一能打动这人的道理。血从他壮实的脸上退了下去。
「看上帝的份上，先生，别急！」他喊道，「我实在万不想冒犯。您知道，先生，我同许多好脾气的、老实巴交的老伙计一样——吠得凶，咬得轻。听我说话，您兴许当我有点犟；哪里，哪里！桑迪·斯普罗特心里是个和气的老头！这人对我的纠缠烦恼，您是想也想不到的。」
「很好，先生，」我说，「那我就不客气领您的情了：请你把德鲁蒙德先生最近的下落见告。」
「欢迎之至，先生！」他说，「至于这位小姐（我向她请安！）——他只怕早把她忘了个干净。这人我摸得透，您瞧；我在他身上早折过钱。他心里除了自己再没有别人；宗族也好，君王也好，女儿也好，只要他自己能吃饱，一概都能扔下！是的，连他的通信人也能扔。因为从某种意义上讲，我几乎就可以算是他的通信人。实情是，我们两个合伙做过一桩生意，我看这桩生意要叫桑迪·斯普罗特大出血了。这人同我的合伙人差不多；我拿名誉给您一句话：他在哪里，我实在一概不知。他兴许会来这赫尔沃特；兴许明天就来，兴许十二个月也不来；什么事我也不奇怪——只奇怪一件事：他要还我钱。您瞧我的处境；很清楚，我不大可能同这位小姐——您是这么叫她吧——有什么牵扯。她不能住在这儿，这是一定的。真的，先生，我是个孤身汉子！我若把她收留下，等那无赖露了面，十之八九要逼我娶她。」
「这种话不必说了，」我说，「我带这位小姐去找更好的朋友。请给我纸笔墨砚，我把我在莱顿的通信地址留下给詹姆斯·莫尔。他可以向我打听到哪里去寻他女儿。」

===Original===

This word I wrote and sealed; which while I was doing, Sprott of his own motion made a welcome offer, to charge himself with Miss Drummond’s mails, and even send a porter for them to the inn. I advanced him to that effect a dollar or two to be a cover, and he gave me an acknowledgment in writing of the sum.
Whereupon (I giving my arm to Catriona) we left the house of this unpalatable rascal. She had said no word throughout, leaving me to judge and speak in her place; I, upon my side, had been careful not to embarrass her by a glance; and even now although my heart still glowed inside of me with shame and anger, I made it my affair to seem quite easy.
“Now,” said I, “let us get back to yon same inn where they can speak the French, have a piece of dinner, and inquire for conveyances to Rotterdam. I will never be easy till I have you safe again in the hands of Mrs. Gebbie.”
“I suppose it will have to be,” said Catriona, “though whoever will be pleased, I do not think it will be her. And I will remind you this once again that I have but one shilling, and three baubees.”
“And just this once again,” said I, “I will remind you it was a blessing that I came alongst with you.”
“What else would I be thinking all this time!” says she, and I thought weighed a little on my arm. “It is you that are the good friend to me.”

===Chinese===

这个字条我写好封上；趁我写字的当口，斯普罗特自己主动做了个漂亮的表示：由他承领德鲁蒙德小姐的行李，还派一个脚夫去客栈取。为此我预付了他一两块钱作抵，他给我写了一张收条。
随后（我把胳膊递给卡特琳娜）我们离开了这个叫人倒胃的恶棍的家。她一路一言不发，全由我替她拤量、替她开口；我呢，也小心着不用一眼看她叫她难堪；就连此刻，我心里还烧着羞和火，也拿定主意装得若无其事。
「好了，」我说，「咱们回方才那家会讲法国话的客栈，吃口饭，打听去鹿特丹的班船。不把你平平安安交还到格比太太手里，我总不放心。」
「看来只好这样了，」卡特琳娜说，「不管谁乐意，反正她不会乐意。我再提醒你一回：我只有一个先令，外加三个小铜子。」
「我也再提醒你一回，」我说，「亏得我跟你一路，是天赐的福气。」
「我这一路心里想的还能是别的么！」她说；我觉得她的手在我臂上沉了一沉。「你才是我真正的好朋友。」

## XIX I Am Much in the Hands of the Ladies / 十九、我大落在太太小姐们手里
===Original===

The copying was a weary business, the more so as I perceived very early there was no sort of urgency in the matters treated, and began very early to consider my employment a pretext. I had no sooner finished, than I got to horse, used what remained of daylight to the best purpose, and being at last fairly benighted, slept in a house by Almond-Water side. I was in the saddle again before the day, and the Edinburgh booths were just opening when I clattered in by the West Bow and drew up a smoking horse at my lord Advocate’s door. I had a written word for Doig, my lord’s private hand that was thought to be in all his secrets, a worthy, little plain man, all fat and snuff and self-sufficiency. Him I found already at his desk and already bedabbled with maccabaw, in the same anteroom where I rencountered with James More. He read the note scrupulously through like a chapter in his Bible.
“H’m,” says he, “ye come a wee thing ahint-hand, Mr. Balfour. The bird’s flaen, we hae letten her out.”
“Miss Drummond is set free?” I cried.
“Achy!” said he. “What would we keep her for, ye ken? To hae made a steer about the bairn would hae pleased naebody.”
“And where’ll she be now?” says I.
“Gude kens!” says Doig, with a shrug.
“She’ll have gone home to Lady Allardyce, I’m thinking,” said I.
“That’ll be it,” said he.
“Then I’ll gang there straight,” says I.
“But ye’ll be for a bite or ye go?” said he.
“Neither bite nor sup,” said I. “I had a good waucht of milk in by Ratho.”
“Aweel, aweel,” says Doig. “But ye’ll can leave your horse here and your bags, for it seems we’re to have your up-put.”
“Na, na,” said I. “Tamson’s mear17 would never be the thing for me, this day of all days.”
Doig speaking somewhat broad, I had been led by imitation into an accent much more countrified than I was usually careful to affect, a good deal broader indeed than I have written it down; and I was the more ashamed when another voice joined in behind me with a scrap of a ballad:

> “Gae saddle me the bonny black,

Gae saddle sune and mak’ him ready,

For I will down the Gatehope-slack,

And a’ to see my bonny leddy.”

===Chinese===

抄写的活计很累人，更累人的是我很早便看穿：所办的事务毫无急切可言，我的差事只是个由头。一抄完，我立刻上马，把剩余的日光用到十足，天黑透了才在阿尔蒙水畔一户人家过夜。天亮前我又上了鞍，爱丁堡的铺子刚开门，我已咔咔地驰过西弓街，在我家检察长大人门前勒住一匹冒烟的马。我带着写给多伊格的字条——大人的私人文书，据说大人的机密全在他肚里——一个矮小敦实的人，一身肥肉、鼻烟和自满。我见他已在案前，鼻烟已沾了满身，就在我撞见詹姆斯·莫尔的那间前厅里。他把字条一字不苟地读了一遍，像读《圣经》里的一章。

「嗯，」他说，「你来得晚了那么一步，鲍尔弗先生。鸟儿飞了，俺们放她走了。」

「德拉蒙德小姐放出来了？」我叫道。

「可不！」他说，「不然俺们留她做什么，你可想？为那丫头闹出动静，谁也不痛快。」

「那她这会儿在哪儿？」我说。

「天知道！」多伊格耸耸肩。

「她多半回阿拉迪斯太太家去了。」我说。

「想必是。」他说。

「那我就直奔那儿。」我说。

「可你不去吃口东西再走？」他说。

「不吃也不喝。拉索那头我灌了一大口奶。」

「也罢，也罢，」多伊格说，「可你总可以把马和包袱留下，看这样子俺们还得管你住呢。」

「不不，」我说，「偏是今天，说什么也不能靠『汤姆森家的马』17代步。」

多伊格口音土得厉害，我跟着学，不知不觉腔调比平日惯常装的乡气得多——实际比写下来的这还要重得多；更叫我臊的是，背后忽然又有一个声音，接了两句歌谣：

> 「给我备上那匹黑骏马，

快快备好，莫要耽搁，

我要下盖特霍普坡去，

只为看我的俊姑娘。」

===Original===

The young lady, when I turned to her, stood in a morning gown, and her hands muffled in the same, as if to hold me at a distance. Yet I could not but think there was kindness in the eye with which she saw me.
“My best respects to you, Mistress Grant,” said I bowing.
“The like to yourself, Mr. David,” she replied, with a deep courtesy, “And I beg to remind you of an old musty saw, that meat and mass never hindered man. The mass I cannot afford you, for we are all good Protestants. But the meat I press on your attention. And I would not wonder but I could find something for your private ear that would be worth the stopping for.”
“Mistress Grant,” said I, “I believe I am already your debtor for some merry words﻿—and I think they were kind too﻿—on a piece of unsigned paper.”
“Unsigned paper?” says she, and made a droll face, which was likewise wondrous beautiful, as of one trying to remember.
“Or else I am the more deceived,” I went on. “But to be sure, we shall have the time to speak of these, since your father is so good as to make me for a while your inmate; and the *gomeral* begs you at this time only for the favour of his liberty.”
“You give yourself hard names,” said she.
“Mr. Doig and I would be blythe to take harder at your clever pen,” says I.
“Once more I have to admire the discretion of all menfolk,” she replied. “But if you will not eat, off with you at once; you will be back the sooner, for you go on a fool’s errand. Off with you, Mr. David,” she continued, opening the door.

> “He has lowpen on his bonny grey,

He rade the richt gate and the ready;

I trow he would neither stint nor stay,

Far he was seeking his bonny leddy.”

===Chinese===

我回过头，见那位年轻小姐站在那里，一身晨衣，两手拢在衣袖里，像是要同我保持距离。可我不能不觉得，她看我的眼神里含着善意。

「小可向格兰特小姐问安。」我躬身说。

「也向戴维先生问安，」她深深一个万福答道，「小女子还要提醒先生一句老掉牙的俗话：饭食和弥撒，从不误人事。弥撒小女子供不起，俺们都是好好的新教徒；饭食可要请先生赏脸。而且我不敢说，小女子就没有几句单给先生一个人听的话，值这一停。」

「格兰特小姐，」我说，「有一张没署名的纸片上几句话说得好耍——我想也是好意——想已欠下小姐的情了。」

「没署名的纸片？」她说，做了个古怪的鬼脸——那鬼脸同时美得出奇——像是苦苦回想。

「要么就是我认错了人，」我接着说，「不过反正往后有的是工夫细谈，令尊既好意留我做些时日的房客；这会儿这个*呆子*只求小姐赏他一个自由身。」

「先生给自己下的名目好重。」她说。

「多伊格先生和我都乐意领教更重的，出自小姐那支巧笔。」我说。

「男子汉个个这份心细，又叫小女子佩服一遭。」她答道，「可先生既不肯吃，就快请吧；去得快，回得也快——先生这是去跑一趟傻瓜差事。快请，戴维先生。」她接着说，推开了门。

> 「他跨上那匹灰骏马，

走的正道，行的直路；

小女子料他不停不歇，

一心寻他的俊姑娘。」

===Original===

I did not wait to be twice bidden, and did justice to Miss Grant’s citation on the way to Dean.
Old Lady Allardyce walked there alone in the garden, in her hat and mutch, and having a silver-mounted staff of some black wood to lean upon. As I alighted from my horse, and drew near to her with congees, I could see the blood come in her face, and her head fling into the air like what I had conceived of empresses.
“What brings you to my poor door?” she cried, speaking high through her nose. “I cannot bar it. The males of my house are dead and buried; I have neither son nor husband to stand in the gate for me; any beggar can pluck me by the baird18﻿—and a baird there is, and that’s the worst of it yet!” she added, partly to herself.
I was extremely put out at this reception, and the last remark, which seemed like a daft wife’s, left me near hand speechless.
“I see I have fallen under your displeasure, ma’am,” said I. “Yet I will still be so bold as ask after Mistress Drummond.”
She considered me with a burning eye, her lips pressed close together into twenty creases, her hand shaking on her staff. “This cows all!” she cried. “Ye come to me to spier for her! Would God I knew!”
“She is not here?” I cried.
She threw up her chin and made a step and a cry at me, so that I fell back incontinent.
“Out upon your leeing throat!” she cried. “What! ye come and spier at me! She’s in jyle, whaur ye took her to﻿—that’s all there is to it. And of a’ the beings ever I beheld in breeks, to think it should be you! Ye timmer scoun’rel, if I had a male left to my name I would have your jaicket dustit till ye raired.”
I thought it not good to delay longer in that place because I remarked her passion to be rising. As I turned to the horse-post she even followed me; and I make no shame to confess that I rode away with the one stirrup on and scrambling for the other.
As I knew no other quarter where I could push my inquiries, there was nothing left me but to return to the Advocate’s. I was well received by the four ladies, who were now in company together, and must give the news of Prestongrange and what word went in the west country, at the most inordinate length and with great weariness to myself; while all the time that young lady, with whom I so much desired to be alone again, observed me quizzically and seemed to find pleasure in the sight of my impatience.

===Chinese===

不用她再催第二遍；去迪恩的路上，我把格兰特小姐的引文好好想了一遍。

老阿拉迪斯太太独自在园子里踱步，戴着帽子和睡帽，拄一根乌木镶银的手杖。我下马趋前行礼，就看出血往她脸上涌，头往空里一扬，俨然我想象中的女皇气派。

「什么风把您吹到我这寒门来了？」她拿腔拿调地喊道。「我又关不上门。我家的男人都死绝埋绝了；没儿没夫替我把门，随便哪个叫花子都能来捋我胡子18——偏生这胡子还真有，这才是最糟的！」后半句一半是对自己说的。

这番迎接弄得我狼狈至极，末了那句更像疯婆子的话，简直叫我张口结舌。

「我看我是惹了太太不痛快，」我说，「可我仍斗胆请问德拉蒙德小姐安在。」

她用喷火的眼睛盯我，嘴唇抿成二十道褶子，手在手杖上发抖。「欺人太甚！」她叫道，「你倒来问我讨她！老天爷，我哪知道！」

「她不在这儿？」我叫起来。

她下巴一扬，抢上一步，冲我一嗓子，吓得我当场倒退。

「你这个扯谎的嗓子！她叫道，「什么！你跑来问我！她在牢里，你送去的那地方——就这么回事。天下穿裤子的活物我见得多了，偏偏是你！你这木头流氓，我名下但凡剩一个男人，非把你外套打个稀烂，叫你嗷嗷叫！」

我看她的火气正往上撞，此地不宜久留。我转身走向拴马桩，她竟还追着骂；我不怕丢人地承认：一只脚踩上镫，另一只还没找着，我就纵马跑了。

我此外想不出还能上哪儿打听，只好回检察长大人家。四位太太小姐正聚在一处，客客气气接待了我，又非要我讲普雷斯顿格兰奇的消息、讲西边各县的传言，讲到天长地久，我烦得要死；那位我一心想再单独谈谈的小姐，始终打趣地望着我，似乎拿我的着急开心。

===Original===

At last, after I had endured a meal with them, and was come very near the point of appealing for an interview before her aunt, she went and stood by the music case, and picking out a tune, sang to it on a high key﻿—“He that will not when he may, When he will he shall have nay.” But this was the end of her rigours, and presently, after making some excuse of which I have no mind, she carried me away in private to her father’s library. I should not fail to say that she was dressed to the nines, and appeared extraordinary handsome.
“Now, Mr. David, sit ye down here and let us have a two-handed crack,” said she. “For I have much to tell you, and it appears besides that I have been grossly unjust to your good taste.”
“In what manner, Mistress Grant?” I asked. “I trust I have never seemed to fail in due respect.”
“I will be your surety, Mr. David,” said she. “Your respect, whether to yourself or your poor neighbours, has been always and most fortunately beyond imitation. But that is by the question. You got a note from me?” she asked.
“I was so bold as to suppose so upon inference,” said I, “and it was kindly thought upon.”
“It must have prodigiously surprised you,” said she. “But let us begin with the beginning. You have not perhaps forgot a day when you were so kind as to escort three very tedious misses to Hope Park? I have the less cause to forget it myself, because you was so particular obliging as to introduce me to some of the principles of the Latin grammar, a thing which wrote itself profoundly on my gratitude.”
“I fear I was sadly pedantical,” said I, overcome with confusion at the memory. “You are only to consider I am quite unused with the society of ladies.”
“I will say the less about the grammar then,” she replied. “But how came you to desert your charge? ‘He has thrown her out, overboard, his ain dear Annie!’ ” she hummed; “and his ain dear Annie and her two sisters had to taigle home by theirselves like a string of green geese! It seems you returned to my papa’s, where you showed yourself excessively martial, and then on to realms unknown, with an eye (it appears) to the Bass Rock; solan geese being perhaps more to your mind than bonny lasses.”
Through all this raillery there was something indulgent in the lady’s eye which made me suppose there might be better coming.

===Chinese===

好不容易捱过一顿饭，眼看就要开口求她当着姨母赐见，她却起身走到乐谱柜前，拣了个调子，扯着尖嗓门唱起来——「能有时候不，偏要等没有，等到要有心，人家也不给。」唱罢，她的刁难到了头；不多时，她寻了个我记不清的由头，把我单独引到她父亲的书房。不得不说，她那天打扮得花枝招展，人显得分外标致。

「好了，戴维先生，坐下，咱们俩好好拉拉。」她说，「我有好多话告诉你；此外看来，我先前对你的眼光大大冤枉了你。」

「怎么讲，格兰特小姐？」我问，「但愿我从没失过敬。」

「我给你作保，戴维先生，」她说，「你的敬意，无论对自身还是对穷街坊，从来多得无人可及，可喜可贺。不过那是题外话。你收到我的条子了？」她问。

「我斗胆凭推断这样猜了，」我说，「那想得周到。」

「想必叫你吃了一惊。」她说，「可咱们从头说起。想必你没忘那一天，你好心护送三位顶乏味的小姐去霍普公园？我更忘不了，因为你老人家好心地给我讲了几条拉丁文法，这事在我感激的心上落了很深一笔。」

「我怕我当时迂得厉害，」我说，回想起来窘得不行，「您只消想想，我全不惯同太太小姐们应酬。」

「那文法我就不多说了。」她答道，「可你怎么把护送的对象丢下就跑了？『他把亲亲的安妮扔了，扔进了海里！』」她哼了一句，「亲亲的安妮和她两个妹妹，只好自己一路磨蹭回家，像一串小绿鹅！听说你折回我爸爸那儿，大显了一通军人气概，随后奔了谁也不知道的去处，一双眼睛（据说）瞄着巴斯岩；梭鲣鸟大约比俊姑娘更合你的意。」

这一路打趣里，小姐的眼睛却含着纵容，叫我猜想后头兴许有好话。

===Original===

“You take a pleasure to torment me,” said I, “and I make a very feckless plaything; but let me ask you to be more merciful. At this time there is but the one thing that I care to hear of, and that will be news of Catriona.”
“Do you call her by that name to her face, Mr. Balfour?” she asked.
“In troth, and I am not very sure,” I stammered.
“I would not do so in any case to strangers,” said Miss Grant. “And why are you so much immersed in the affairs of this young lady?”
